For other uses, see [Yemeni civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_civil_war_\(disambiguation\)).

| Part of the [Yemeni crisis](45326059.md) and the [Iran–Saudi Arabia proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_war) |  |  |
| Political and military control in Yemen as of 13 September 2026:   [Presidential Leadership Council](70488867.md) (PLC) (since 2022)   [Supreme Political Council](51279323.md) (SPC) (since 2016)   [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) (AQAP) (since 2009) |  |  |
| Date16 September 2014 – present (12 years, 2 weeks and 3 days)Location[Yemen](350939.md) and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)Status [Ongoing](2346678.md) [Houthis take control of Sanaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sanaa_\(2014\)) (2014) [Saudi Arabian-led coalition forces intervene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian%E2%80%93led_intervention_in_Yemen) from 26 March 2015, backing the [Hadi-led government](31167279.md) Former President [Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh) is assassinated in the [Battle of Sanaa (2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sanaa_\(2017\)) [United Nations](31769.md) announce two-month nationwide truce on 2 April 2022; further extended on 2 June and officially expired in October[^cite_note-1] [Hadi-led government](31167279.md) resigns (7 April 2022) Transfer of power to the [Presidential Leadership Council](70488867.md) (PLC), consisting of [Southern Transitional Council](54251566.md) (STC) representatives[^cite_note-CNN-2][^cite_note-3][^cite_note-4] [Rashad al-Alimi](70502008.md) becomes [President](444090.md) and STC President [Aidarus al-Zoubaidi](52145652.md) becomes [Vice President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_Yemen) [STC](54251566.md) launches [an offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Southern_Yemen_offensive) against the [Islah party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Islah_\(Yemen\)), taking large amounts of territory (August 2022) Negotiations to end the civil war that include all major combatants[^cite_note-5] start in April 2023 after Iran and Saudi Arabia resume [diplomatic relations](19661342.md)[^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7] [Red Sea crisis](75207766.md) begins in 2023 leading to [Operation Prosperity Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Prosperity_Guardian), [American–British attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93UK_airstrikes_on_Yemen), [further American attacks,](79465918.md) and [Israeli attacks](79886126.md) STC launches [an offensive](81757459.md) against government forces in December 2025, seizing the [Hadhramaut](3604476.md) and [al-Mahrah](2488921.md) governorates Government [counteroffensive](81757459.md#Yemeni_government_counter-offensive) retakes Hadhramaut and al-Mahrah governorates in January 2026; STC subsequently dissolves [2026 Yemen offensives](84167596.md) begin in September 2026 |  |  |

The **Yemeni civil war** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الحرب الأهلية اليمنية, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *al-ḥarb al-ʾahlīyah al-yamanīyah*) is an ongoing multilateral [civil war](7085.md) that began in late 2014 mainly between the [Rashad al-Alimi](70502008.md)-led [Presidential Leadership Council](70488867.md) and the [Mahdi al-Mashat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_al-Mashat)-led [Supreme Political Council](51279323.md), along with their respective supporters and allies. Both sides claim to constitute the official government of [Yemen](350939.md) while the Presidential Leadership Council is recognized by the United Nations as the official government.[^cite_note-102]

The civil war began in September 2014 when [Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_movement) forces [took over the capital city Sanaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sanaa_\(2014\)), which was followed by a rapid [Houthi takeover of the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_takeover_in_Yemen). On 21 March 2015, the Houthi-led [Supreme Revolutionary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Revolutionary_Committee) declared a general mobilization to overthrow then-president [Abdrabbuh Mansur Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdrabbuh_Mansur_Hadi) and expand their control by driving into southern provinces.[^cite_note-103] The Houthi offensive, allied with military forces loyal to former Yemeni president [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh), began fighting the next day in [Lahij Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahij_Governorate). By 25 March, [Lahij](2480600.md) fell to the Houthis and they reached the outskirts of Aden, the seat of power for Hadi's government.[^cite_note-104] Hadi fled the country the same day.[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-105][^cite_note-106] Concurrently, a [coalition led by Saudi Arabia](46230181.md) launched military operations by using air strikes and restored the former [Yemeni government](78626.md).[^cite_note-offensive-107] Although there has been no direct intervention by the Iranian government in Yemen, the civil war is widely regarded as part of the [Iran–Saudi proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict).[^cite_note-108]

Houthi insurgents currently control the capital [Sanaa](18691665.md) and all of former [North Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemen_Arab_Republic) except for eastern [Marib Governorate](3604391.md). After the formation of the [Southern Transitional Council](54251566.md) (STC) in 2017 and the subsequent [capture of Aden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_\(2018\)) by the STC forces in 2018, the pro-republican forces became fractured, with regular clashes between pro-Hadi forces backed by [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) and southern separatists backed by the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md).[^cite_note-109] [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) (AQAP) and the [Islamic State](9087364.md) have also carried out attacks against both factions, with AQAP controlling swathes of territory in the hinterlands, and along stretches of the coast.[^cite_note-110]

The UN brokered a two-month nationwide truce on 2 April 2022 between Yemen's warring parties, which allowed fuel imports into Houthi-held areas and some flights to operate from [Sanaa International Airport](4202141.md) to Jordan and Egypt.[^cite_note-Ghobari-2022-111] On 7 April 2022, the Hadi government was dissolved and the [Presidential Leadership Council](70488867.md) (PLC) took command of the Yemeni Republic, incorporating the Southern Transitional Council into its new government. The UN announced on 2 June 2022 that the nationwide truce had been further extended by two months.[^cite_note-al-Sakani-2022-112] According to the UN, over 150,000 people have been killed in Yemen,[^cite_note-113] as well as estimates of more than 227,000 dead as a result of an [ongoing famine](51686838.md) and lack of healthcare facilities due to the war.[^cite_note-AlJazeera-20222-114][^cite_note-BBC-20222-115][^cite_note-116] *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported in March 2023 that Iran agreed to halt all military support to the Houthis and abide by the [UN arms embargo](5974443.md), as part of a Chinese-brokered [Iran-Saudi rapprochement deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict#Iran-Saudi_Arabia_Rapprochement). The agreement is viewed as part of Saudi Arabian-led efforts to pressure the Houthi militants to end the conflict through negotiated settlement; with Saudi and U.S. officials describing the concomitant Iranian behaviour as a "litmus test" for the endurance of the Chinese-brokered [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente).[^cite_note-117] Since then, however, Iran has maintained military and logistical support for the Houthis.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] On 23 December 2023, [Hans Grundberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Grundberg), the UN special envoy for Yemen, announced that the warring parties had committed to steps towards a ceasefire.[^cite_note-120]

The Saudi-led coalition's bombing of civilian areas has received condemnation from the international community.[^cite_note-121] According to the Yemen Data Project, the bombing campaign has killed or injured an estimated 19,196 civilians as of March 2022.[^cite_note-122] Houthi drone attacks targeting civilian areas in Saudi Arabia, [UAE](69328.md), and Southern Yemen have also attracted global condemnation;[^cite_note-123] and the [UN Security Council](31956.md) has imposed a global [arms embargo](5974443.md) on the Houthis since 2015.[^cite_note-124] The [United States](3434750.md) has provided intelligence and logistical support for the Saudi Arabian-led campaign,[^cite_note-nytimes.com-125] and despite an earlier pledge to withdraw [U.S. support for Saudi Arabia in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._support_for_Saudi_Arabian-led_operations_in_Yemen), the [Biden administration](65732174.md) announced in 2022 the sale of weapons to the Saudi Arabian-led coalition.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127]

## Historical context

[Islam in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Yemen) predominantly takes the forms of [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) and [Shia](26961.md) Islam.[^cite_note-BritannicaYemen-128] Among the Shia, the Ismaili and Zaydi sects [ historically competed for influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islam_in_Yemen).[^cite_note-SanaCenterIsmaili-129] Following the fall of the [Ismaili states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurayids) ruled by the Hamdan tribe in the 11th century, the [Ayyubid expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty) led to the majority of the Hamdan tribe converting to [Zaydism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaydism), a Shia sect.[^cite_note-\:0-130]

During the [ Rasulid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasulid_dynasty) period (1229 to 1454), [Shafi'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi'i_school) Sunni Islam gained popularity in Yemen.[^cite_note-Pulkkinen-131] Over time, the Zaydis in the north and the Rasulid rulers in the south competed for dominance. This rivalry diminished following the decline of Rasulid rule and the increasing involvement of imperial powers, including the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-\:0-130]

The defeat of the Ottomans in the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War) eventually brought about the first (incomplete) unification of Yemen. This became possible during the rule of the Hamid al-Din dynasty under [Imam Yahya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Muhammad_Hamid_ed-Din) (r. 1918–1948) and his son [Ahmad bin Yahya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_bin_Yahya) (r. 1948–1962). The unification also meant the establishment of a hierarchy within which Zaydis had authoritative power over Sunnis.[^cite_note-\:0-130] As a consequence, the difference in status became institutionalized and normalized. The Hamid al-Din dynasty faced discontent among the Zaydis due to non-conformity to Zaydi traditions; the dynasty came to an end with the overthrow of Imam Yahya's grandson, [Imam Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Badr), in 1962. There followed a [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Yemen_Civil_War) (1962 to 1970) in which republicans opposed the royalists. Helen Lackner explains that such a division was not ideological; it was based on past feuds. Nonetheless, external powers like Saudi Arabia intervened for ideological motives: the Saudis sought to maintain the monarchical system to which they adhered themselves. Despite Saudi Arabia's original intentions, in 1970, the republican form was retained.[^cite_note-\:0-130]



Yemen prior to the 1990 unification

While these developments took place in the North, liberation movements against British presence in [Aden Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden_Colony) and the [Aden Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden_Protectorate) broke out, resulting to the formation in 1967 of a socialist state, (and hence a Soviet ally) in the Southern Arabian region. It became known as the [People's Democratic Republic of Yemen](718749.md) (PDRY) from 1970.[^cite_note-\:0-130]

The PDRY was involved in the internal disputes of its northern counterpart. It had previously supported the republicans in fighting off Saudi Arabia's influence, but it was the [Hamdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_al-Hamdi) regime (1974 to 1977) in the Yemen Arab Republic (North Yemen) that was most notorious for its policies that aimed to reduce Saudi presence in Yemeni politics. Hamdi's rule was, nonetheless, short-lived. A period of political unrest marked by the assassination of former presidents led to [Colonel Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh)'s rise to the presidency of the Yemen Arab Republic. Saleh's rule lasted for 33 years, from 1978 until 2012. The major event that marked his regime was the [unification of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_unification) in 1990. His attempt was not entirely successful as a [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_civil_war_\(1994\)) broke out in 1994. However, he was able to come out of it victorious. The Republic of Yemen became [ constitutionally established](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Yemen) in 1991.[^cite_note-\:0-130]

Saleh tried to maintain his position through constitutional amendments, hoping for his son to take over, but strong opposition and protests pressured him out of office by 2012. Meanwhile, the group "Friends of Yemen" was created, initially to stop al-Qaeda on the Arabian Peninsula (AQAP) after evidence pointed at it as the main perpetrator behind multiple attacks on US military bases and equipment. The "Friends of Yemen" group became more involved in Yemen during the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) of 2010 to 2012, after the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) (GCC) and United Nations recommended putting the management of Yemen's "crisis" into the hands of global actors.[^cite_note-\:5-132]

## The Houthis

Main articles: [Houthis](17240332.md), [Yemeni crisis](45326059.md), and [Houthi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_insurgency)

After the end of their rule, from the 1960s onwards, Zaydis faced discrimination and *Sunnification* policies from the consequent Sunni dominated governments. For example, [Salafis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi) in [Saada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saada) claimed [al-Shawkani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shawkani) as an intellectual precursor, and future Yemeni regimes would uphold his Sunnization policies as a unifier of the country[^cite_note-133] and to undermine Zaydi Shi'ism.[^cite_note-134]

Ansar Allah (sometimes Anglicised as Ansarullah), known popularly as the [Houthis](17240332.md), is a Zaydi group with its origins in the mountainous [Sa'dah Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa'dah_Governorate) on Yemen's northern border with [Saudi Arabia](349303.md). They led a low-level [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_insurgency_in_Yemen) against the [Yemeni government](78626.md) in 2004[^cite_note-135] after their leader, [Hussein Badreddin al-Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_Badreddin_al-Houthi), was killed in a government military crackdown[^cite_note-IRIN-136][^cite_note-\:4-137] following his protests against government policies.[^cite_note-\:2-138][^cite_note-139]

The intensity of the conflict waxed and waned over the course of the 2000s, with multiple peace agreements being negotiated and later disregarded.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] The Houthi insurgency [heated up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Scorched_Earth) in 2009, briefly drawing neighboring Saudi Arabia to the side of the Yemeni government, but cooled the following year after a ceasefire was signed.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

Then during the early stages of the [Yemeni Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Revolution) in 2011, Houthi leader [Abdul-Malik al-Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Malik_al-Houthi) declared the group's support for demonstrations calling for the resignation of President [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh).[^cite_note-144] Later that year, as Saleh prepared to leave office, the Houthis [laid siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Dammaj) to the [Salafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism)-majority village of [Dammaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dammaj) in northern Yemen, a step toward attaining virtual autonomy for Sa'dah.[^cite_note-145]

The Houthis boycotted a [single-candidate election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Yemeni_presidential_election) in early 2012 meant to give [Abdrabbuh Mansur Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdrabbuh_Mansur_Hadi) a two-year term of office.[^cite_note-146] They participated in a [National Dialogue Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Dialogue_Conference_\(Yemen\)), but withheld support from a final accord in early 2014 that extended Hadi's mandate in office for another year.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-cabinetandhadi-148]

Conflict between the Houthis and Sunni tribes in northern Yemen spread to other governorates, including the [Sanaa Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanaa_Governorate) by mid-2014.[^cite_note-149]

### Beginning of the conflict (2014–2015)

Main articles: [Battle of Sanaa (2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sanaa_\(2014\)) and [Houthi takeover in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_takeover_in_Yemen)

See also: [Battle of Aden Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_Airport) and [2015 Sanaa mosque bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Sanaa_mosque_bombings_\(disambiguation\))

After several weeks of street protests against the Hadi administration, which made cuts to fuel subsidies that were unpopular with the group, the Houthis fought the [Yemen Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemen_Army) forces under the command of General [Ali Mohsen al-Ahmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Mohsen_al-Ahmar). In a [battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sanaa_\(2014\)) that lasted only a few days, Houthi fighters seized control of [Sanaa](18691665.md), the Yemeni capital, in September 2014.[^cite_note-150] The Houthis forced President [Abdrabbuh Mansur Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdrabbuh_Mansur_Hadi) to negotiate an agreement to end the violence, in which the government resigned and the Houthis gained an unprecedented level of influence over state institutions and politics.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]

In January 2015, unhappy with a [proposal to split the country into six federal regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalization_of_Yemen),[^cite_note-153] [Houthi fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_Movement) [seized the presidential compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_Yemeni_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in [Sanaa](18691665.md). The power play prompted the resignation of President Hadi and his ministers.[^cite_note-cabinetandhadi-148][^cite_note-shifting-154] The Houthi political leadership then announced the dissolution of [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Yemen\)) and the formation of a [Revolutionary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Committee_\(Yemen\)) to govern the country on 6 February 2015.[^cite_note-155]

On 21 February, one month after Houthi militants confined Hadi to his residence in Sanaa, he slipped out of the capital and traveled to [Aden](38050.md). In a televised address from his hometown, he declared that the Houthi takeover was illegitimate and indicated he remained the constitutional president of Yemen.[^cite_note-plans-156][^cite_note-unconst-157][^cite_note-malay-158] His predecessor as president, [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh)—who had been widely suspected of aiding the [Houthis](17240332.md) during their takeover of Sanaa the previous year—publicly denounced Hadi and called on him to go into exile.[^cite_note-exile-159]

On 19 March 2015, troops loyal to Hadi clashed with those who refused to recognize his authority in the [Battle of Aden Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_Airport). The forces under General [Abdul-Hafez al-Saqqaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Hafez_al-Saqqaf) were defeated, and al-Saqqaf fled toward Sanaa.[^cite_note-160] In apparent retaliation for the routing of al-Saqqaf, warplanes reportedly flown by Houthi pilots bombed Hadi's compound in Aden.[^cite_note-161]

After the 20 March 2015 [Sanaa mosque bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2015_Sanaa_mosque_bombings), in a televised speech, [Abdul-Malik al-Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Malik_al-Houthi), the leader of the Houthis, said his group's decision to mobilize for war was "imperative" under current circumstances and that [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) and its affiliates—among whom he counts Hadi—would be targeted, as opposed to southern Yemen and its citizens.[^cite_note-162] President Hadi declared Aden to be Yemen's temporary capital while Sanaa remained under Houthi control.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

Also, the same day as the mosque bombings, al-Qaeda militants captured the provincial capital of [Lahij](2480600.md), [Al Houta District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Houta_District) after killing about 20 soldiers before being driven out several hours later.[^cite_note-165]

Hadi reiterated in a speech on 21 March 2015 that he was the legitimate president of Yemen and declared, "We will restore security to the country and hoist the flag of Yemen in Sanaa, instead of the Iranian flag."[^cite_note-166] He also declared Aden to be Yemen's "economic and temporary capital" due to the Houthi occupation of Sanaa, which he pledged would be retaken.[^cite_note-capital-167]

In [Sanaa](18691665.md), the Houthi [Revolutionary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Committee_\(Yemen\)) appointed Major General [Hussein Khairan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_Khairan) as Yemen's new defence minister and placed him in overall command of the military offensive.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-bloomberg-169]

### 2025–2026

#### STC offensive and PLC counter-offensive

Main article: [2025–2026 southern Yemen campaign](81757459.md)

On 2 December 2025, the [Southern Transitional Council](54251566.md) (STC) launched a large-scale offensive with a force of 10,000 fighters against Yemeni government positions in the [Hadhramaut Governorate](3604476.md), eventually expanding into the [Al-Mahrah Governorate](2488921.md) and other adjacent governorates. By 8 December, STC forces had seized control of nearly all the governorates that were once part of [South Yemen](718749.md), in addition to [Dhale Governorate](3604352.md), including the entire southern coastline of Yemen, the border region with [Oman](22316.md), and the oil fields in Hadhramaut Governorate.[^cite_note-seizure-170][^cite_note-fault_line2-171] The STC claimed control over all 8 governorates by 9 December.[^cite_note-172] By 9 December, STC forces were estimated to control 90–95% of the populated areas in the former South Yemen, in addition to holding at least 80% of Yemen's proven oil reserves. Of all the regions seized by the STC, the Hadhramaut Governorate was the most strategically valuable one by far.[^cite_note-fault_line2-171] This was seen as marking a major victory for the UAE, and a significant setback for Saudi ambitions in the region, as it could have provided the conditions for the STC eventually declaring the [secession](144732.md) of southern Yemen.

During the offensive, Saudi Arabia withdrew its remaining forces from [Aden](38050.md) and [Aden International Airport](9421129.md).[^cite_note-seizure-170][^cite_note-tighten_grip-173]

Tensions escalated further in late December. Saudi Arabia demanded STC withdrawal from the seized territories and conducted airstrikes on STC positions starting 26 December, including reported strikes in Wadi Nahb (Hadhramaut) and later on a port shipment in Mukalla alleged to contain UAE-supplied weapons.[^cite_note-174] On 30 December 2025, the PLC government, backed by Saudi Arabia, declared a 90-day state of emergency across Yemen, canceled a joint defense agreement with the United Arab Emirates (UAE), ordered the withdrawal of all remaining UAE forces within 24 hours, and imposed a temporary blockade on entry points. The UAE subsequently announced the full withdrawal of its remaining counterterrorism personnel from Yemen, effectively ending its direct military presence and marking a de facto removal from the Saudi-led coalition's operations in the country.[^cite_note-withdraw-175]

On 30 December, the [Royal Saudi Air Force](570367.md) carried out airstrikes on the STC-controlled port city of [Mukalla](2414800.md), targeting what it said was a shipment of weapons that had arrived from the UAE.[^cite_note-176] The UAE denied that the shipments contained weapons.[^cite_note-withdraw-175] Shortly afterward, the internationally recognized Yemeni government ordered all UAE forces to leave Yemeni territory within 24 hours, announced a 72-hour air, land, and sea blockade, and declared a 90-day [state of emergency](298608.md).[^cite_note-177] Later that day, the UAE announced it would voluntarily withdraw its remaining forces from Yemen following the Saudi strikes.[^cite_note-178]

On 2 January 2026, Yemeni government forces, supported by Saudi airstrikes, [launched a counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025%E2%80%932026_Southern_Yemen_campaign) to retake the territories captured by the STC. The STC later announced plans to hold an independence referendum within two years.[^cite_note-179] Backed by Saudi Arabia, government forces advanced rapidly, capturing Seiyun on 3 January and Mukalla the following day, reversing the STC gains.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-mukalla-181] On 7 January, government forces began entering the STC's capital, [Aden](38050.md), amid the collapse of STC resistance.[^cite_note-aden1-182][^cite_note-aden2-183] Subsequently, STC leader [Aidarus al-Zoubaidi](52145652.md) was removed from his position on the [Yemeni Presidential Council](70488867.md), charged with [treason](31292.md), and later fled to the UAE.[^cite_note-stclead1-184][^cite_note-stclead2-185] On 9 January, the STC announced its dissolution following widespread territorial losses across the country.[^cite_note-dissolved2-186][^cite_note-dissolved-187]

#### Involvement in the Iran war

Main articles: [Saudi-led intervention in the Yemeni civil war](46230181.md), [Red Sea crisis](75207766.md), and [2026 Iran war](82533582.md)

Since the 2026 Iran war began, the Houthis have taken significant endeavors to impede the Saudis and other adversaries both on land and sea.

On 3 July 2026, the Houthis said that they confronted a Saudi aircraft while it tried to block an Iranian plane from landing in [Sanaa](18691665.md).[^cite_note-188] On 4 July, the Yemeni government alleged that Houthi snipers had attacked them in [Hays district](29273934.md). The Yemeni government also claimed to have killed 50 Houthi fighters in their alleged "counterattack", while suffering 16 dead and 22 injured of their own in the alleged Houthi "attack".[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] On 13 July, the [defense ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_\(Yemen\)) announced that it [struck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Sana'a_airport_strike) [Sanaa International Airport](4202141.md)'s runway to ‌prevent an Iranian aircraft from landing. The Houthis accused Saudi Arabia of conducting the attack.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] The Houthis later fire missiles towards [Abha International Airport](9419420.md) in Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-193] On 14 July, the Houthis claimed to have shot down a Saudi reconnaissance drone.[^cite_note-194] On 16 July, the pro-government force [Giants brigades](59327857.md) said that they intercepted a boat carrying weapons manufacturing equipment for the Houthis.[^cite_note-195] On 20 July, the Houthis declared a blockade of Saudi Arabian ports and ships in the [Bab-el-Mandeb](62138.md),[^cite_note-196] On the same day, clashes between the Houthis and Yemeni Government forces were recorded in [Hodeidah](769308.md).[^cite_note-197] On 23 July, the Houthis claimed responsibility for strikes against two Saudi tankers in the [Red Sea](26191.md).[^cite_note-198] On 24 July, Saudi Arabia carries out attacks on [Hudaydah Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudaydah_Port) and facilities owned by the state telecommunications corporation in [Hodeidah](769308.md) and [Kamaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamaran).[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] On 25 July, a [Greek air defence system](363254.md) in Saudi Arabia downed two ballistic missiles fired from Yemen.[^cite_note-201] The [Yemeni Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Air_Force) conducted strikes on Houthi missile and drone firing sites in [Marib](3604484.md) and [Al-Jawf Governorate](3604364.md).[^cite_note-202] The Houthis stated that they conducted operations against [Saudi Aramco](290527.md) facilities in [Jizan](1907222.md) and [Yanbu](629638.md).[^cite_note-203] On 26 July, the Houthis shot down a Saudi UAV.[^cite_note-204] On 27 July, Saudi Arabia stated that [Iran-backed Iraqi militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_Iraq) fired drones towards oil facilities in the [Eastern Province](468721.md), and [Riyadh](51554.md).[^cite_note-205] The Houthis stated that they launched attack on Saudi crude oil transport infrastructure with drones.[^cite_note-206] On 28 July, the Houthis claim to have targeted a Saudi ship using ballistic missiles.[^cite_note-207] On 30 July, the [Multinational Maritime Defense Coalition (MMDC)](83826718.md) is established, a 14 country military alliance, to ensure that globally vital waterways surrounding the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) — he [Gulf of Aden](58716.md), the [Strait of [Kuwait](7515890.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Qatar](8238258.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), [Somalia](27358.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Turkey](11125639.md) and [Republic of Yemen](350939.md). On 4 August, the Houthis claimed to have struck a Saudi target in the vicinity of [Najran Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najran_Regional_Airport).[^cite_note-209] Indian Shipping vessel in the [Red Sea](26191.md) and the [Gulf of Aden](58716.md).[^cite_note-211] On 6 August, at least 58 Yemeni soldiers were killed in Houthi attacks.[^cite_note-212] A Houthi missile attack in [Najran Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najran_Province) injured 11 civilians.[^cite_note-213] On 7 August, eight Yemeni soldiers were killed and 12 injured in a Houthi attack in [Marib
